Signature Price Tracker JAH-COTYS-000001 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Price Tracker is a Signature design-study for a price-tracking tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design tracks a fixed weekly grocery basket, prices it every week from a chosen source, and shows the nominal price, the price in 2024 dollars, and the model's projected price for 2030, 2040, and 2050. The model basket cost $86.40 a week in 2024, $98.45 in 2030, $122.38 in 2040, and $152.14 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Price Tracker is a design for a tool that follows one weekly grocery basket, the same basket every week, and shows what it costs today, what it cost last year, and what it would cost in the model years 2030, 2040, and 2050. The basket in this design holds a loaf of bread, a gallon of milk, a dozen eggs, a pound of bacon, a pound of ground beef, a pound of butter, and a pound of cheddar, and it cost $86.40 a week in the 2024 model anchor. The tracker was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records: the public records hold the real history, and the tracker shows how to watch the present.

Description

The Signature Price Tracker is a Signature design-study for a price-tracking tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design tracks a fixed weekly grocery basket, prices it every week from a chosen source, and shows the nominal price, the price in 2024 dollars, and the model's projected price for 2030, 2040, and 2050. The model basket cost $86.40 a week in 2024, $98.45 in 2030, $122.38 in 2040, and $152.14 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a household watch the price of its own weekly shop, see whether the rise is general inflation or one item running away, and know what the basket would cost in the model years without mistaking a model for a forecast?

The Worked Solution

The design fixes one basket and prices it every week from the same source, so the change in the total is the change in prices, not the change in what was bought. The basket is priced in nominal dollars and in 2024 dollars, using the CPI-U for real years and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The series is 2024: $86.40 per week; 2030: $98.45 per week; 2040: $122.38 per week; 2050: $152.14 per week. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_week_first": 86.4, "per_week_last": 152.14, "per_week_multiple": 1.76, "per_week_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model basket cost $86.40 a week in 2024 and $152.14 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model basket costs about $84.62, so the model says the basket stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $86.40 per week; 2030: $98.45 per week; 2040: $122.38 per week; 2050: $152.14 per week. From 2024 to 2050 the per week price rose from $86.40 to $152.14, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real tracker would also need to handle substitutions, store changes, and the fact that households change what they buy.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per week price rose from $86.40 to $152.14, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. The tracker uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. Scientific relation: Signature model deflator, design-study values (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0). The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. The cited background sources confirm the real historical anchors the design builds on; the model values themselves are checked only for internal consistency.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 86.4, 'per week'), (2030, 98.45, 'per week'), (2040, 122.38, 'per week'), (2050, 152.14, 'per week')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignaturePriceTrackerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 86.4, 'per week'), (2030, 98.45, 'per week'), (2040, 122.38, 'per week'), (2050, 152.14, 'per week')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignaturePriceTrackerProgram().run())

Signature Inflation Adjuster JAH-COTYS-000002 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Inflation Adjuster is a Signature design-study for an inflation-adjusting tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a dollar amount and a year, divides by the deflator for that year, and multiplies by 313.7, the CPI-U annual average for 2024. For historical years it uses the real CPI-U; for projected years it uses the Signature model deflator. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Inflation Adjuster is a design for a tool that takes any dollar amount from any year and restates it in 2024 dollars, so that a hundred dollars from 1950 can be compared with a hundred dollars from 2024. The design's worked example restates $100.00: in the model years it becomes $113.95 in 2030, $141.65 in 2040, and $176.09 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The adjuster was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records, which restate every price in 2024 dollars the same way.

Description

The Signature Inflation Adjuster is a Signature design-study for an inflation-adjusting tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a dollar amount and a year, divides by the deflator for that year, and multiplies by 313.7, the CPI-U annual average for 2024. For historical years it uses the real CPI-U; for projected years it uses the Signature model deflator. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a reader compare a price from 1950 with a price from 2024, when the dollar itself changed, and how can the same arithmetic be extended to the model years without pretending the model is a measurement?

The Worked Solution

The design restates any amount with one formula: amount in 2024 dollars equals the amount times 313.7 divided by the deflator for its year. The deflator is the real CPI-U annual average for a historical year and the Signature model deflator for a projected year. The series is 2024: $100.00 dollars; 2030: $113.95 dollars; 2040: $141.65 dollars; 2050: $176.09 dollars. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "dollars_first": 100.0, "dollars_last": 176.09, "dollars_multiple": 1.76, "dollars_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model $100.00 of 2024 becomes $113.95 in 2030, $141.65 in 2040, and $176.09 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model $176.09 is about $97.94, close to $100.00, because the model deflator grows slightly faster than the model rate by design. The series is 2024: $100.00 dollars; 2030: $113.95 dollars; 2040: $141.65 dollars; 2050: $176.09 dollars. From 2024 to 2050 the dollars price rose from $100.00 to $176.09, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

  • MealFan - Grocery Price Index 2026 (BLS Average Price Data) — https://www.mealfan.com/grocery-price-index/ (BLS average price data, the background data for the adjuster's examples.)

History

The Signature Inflation Adjuster is a design for a tool that takes any dollar amount from any year and restates it in 2024 dollars, so that a hundred dollars from 1950 can be compared with a hundred dollars from 2024. The design's worked example restates $100.00: in the model years it becomes $113.95 in 2030, $141.65 in 2040, and $176.09 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The adjuster was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records, which restate every price in 2024 dollars the same way.

Problem

How can a reader compare a price from 1950 with a price from 2024, when the dollar itself changed, and how can the same arithmetic be extended to the model years without pretending the model is a measurement?

Method

The design restates any amount with one formula: amount in 2024 dollars equals the amount times 313.7 divided by the deflator for its year. The deflator is the real CPI-U annual average for a historical year and the Signature model deflator for a projected year. The series is 2024: $100.00 dollars; 2030: $113.95 dollars; 2040: $141.65 dollars; 2050: $176.09 dollars. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

Signature model deflator, design-study values (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0)

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. The cited background sources confirm the real historical anchors the design builds on; the model values themselves are checked only for internal consistency.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The model $100.00 of 2024 becomes $113.95 in 2030, $141.65 in 2040, and $176.09 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model $176.09 is about $97.94, close to $100.00, because the model deflator grows slightly faster than the model rate by design. The series is 2024: $100.00 dollars; 2030: $113.95 dollars; 2040: $141.65 dollars; 2050: $176.09 dollars. From 2024 to 2050 the dollars price rose from $100.00 to $176.09, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

Skeptical analysis

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real inflation jumps, stalls, and falls, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real adjuster would also need to warn that the CPI-U measures an average basket, not any one household's basket.

Specification

  • dimensions: model price in US dollars per dollars; model years 2024-2050; Signature model deflator points
  • care: Never present a model value as a real historical price or a forecast; keep the design-study label on every display of the series.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One 1 Signature inflation adjuster in the design: what the model charges and why.
  • A2: The whole model series: the design across its decades.
  • A3: The design against the whole economy: model prices restated in 2024 dollars.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 100.0, 'dollars'), (2030, 113.95, 'dollars'), (2040, 141.65, 'dollars'), (2050, 176.09, 'dollars')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Run 2 SHA-256: b10a2b2f0cfd5720a26a8e6af83d227242d874f4462133f57f7696429b40f411

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureInflationAdjusterProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 100.0, 'dollars'), (2030, 113.95, 'dollars'), (2040, 141.65, 'dollars'), (2050, 176.09, 'dollars')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureInflationAdjusterProgram().run())

Signature Cost Visualizer JAH-COTYS-000003 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Cost Visualizer is a Signature design-study for a price-visualization tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design draws three lines: the nominal model price of a loaf of bread, the same price in 2024 dollars, and the real historical bread price from the public record. The model loaf cost $1.97 in 2024, $2.24 in 2030, $2.79 in 2040, and $3.47 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Cost Visualizer is a design for a tool that draws the price of a loaf of bread as a picture: a line that climbs from $1.97 a loaf in 2024 to $2.24 in 2030, $2.79 in 2040, and $3.47 in 2050, with a second line showing the same prices in 2024 dollars, nearly flat by design, and a third line showing the real historical bread prices from 1930 to 2024 behind them. The visualizer was designed as a Signature companion to the public bread record, which holds the real history the picture is drawn from.

Description

The Signature Cost Visualizer is a Signature design-study for a price-visualization tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design draws three lines: the nominal model price of a loaf of bread, the same price in 2024 dollars, and the real historical bread price from the public record. The model loaf cost $1.97 in 2024, $2.24 in 2030, $2.79 in 2040, and $3.47 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a price series be drawn so that a reader sees at a glance whether bread is getting dearer or only the dollar is getting smaller, and how can the model years be drawn without looking like a prediction?

The Worked Solution

The design draws the nominal model price as a rising line and the 2024-dollar price as a flat line, so the gap between them is the inflation the model assumes. The historical real prices are drawn in a different color and labeled as real, and the model years are drawn dashed and labeled as model, so no reader mistakes the dashed line for a forecast. The series is 2024: $1.97 per loaf; 2030: $2.24 per loaf; 2040: $2.79 per loaf; 2050: $3.47 per loaf. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_loaf_first": 1.97, "per_loaf_last": 3.47, "per_loaf_multiple": 1.76, "per_loaf_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model loaf cost $1.97 in 2024 and $3.47 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model loaf costs about $1.93, so the model says the loaf stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $1.97 per loaf; 2030: $2.24 per loaf; 2040: $2.79 per loaf; 2050: $3.47 per loaf. From 2024 to 2050 the per loaf price rose from $1.97 to $3.47, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real visualizer would also need to handle the unit change in the milk series and the gaps in the butter series, not just the clean bread series.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per loaf price rose from $1.97 to $3.47, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. series = [(2024, 1.97, 'per loaf'), (2030, 2.24, 'per loaf'), (2040, 2.79, 'per loaf'), (2050, 3.47, 'per loaf')]

        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}

        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]

        out[key + '_last'] = p1

        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}

        if y in cpi:

    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv

class SignatureCostVisualizerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 1.97, 'per loaf'), (2030, 2.24, 'per loaf'), (2040, 2.79, 'per loaf'), (2050, 3.47, 'per loaf')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureCostVisualizerProgram().run())

Signature Then-vs-Now Comparator JAH-COTYS-000004 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Then-vs-Now Comparator is a Signature design-study for a then-versus-now comparison tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design assembles one basket from the real BLS prices of 1950 and 2024, shows the nominal multiple and the price in 2024 dollars for each year, and projects the basket to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The 1950 and 2024 basket figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Then-vs-Now Comparator is a design for a tool that takes a shopping basket from 1950, prices the same basket in 2024, and shows what it would cost in the model year 2050. The design's basket cost $12.40 in 1950, $186.20 in 2024, and $327.90 in the 2050 model, where the 1950 and 2024 figures are assembled from the real BLS prices in the public records and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The comparator was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records, which hold the real prices the basket is built from.

Description

The Signature Then-vs-Now Comparator is a Signature design-study for a then-versus-now comparison tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design assembles one basket from the real BLS prices of 1950 and 2024, shows the nominal multiple and the price in 2024 dollars for each year, and projects the basket to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The 1950 and 2024 basket figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a reader see what a 1950 shop would cost today, and what it would cost in the model year 2050, without mixing the real prices with the model price?

The Worked Solution

The design assembles one basket from the real BLS prices of the public records for 1950 and 2024, converts each basket into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, and then grows the 2024 basket forward to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The real figures and the model figure are labeled separately on every display. The series is 1950: $12.40 model basket; 2024: $186.20 model basket; 2050: $327.90 model basket. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 3, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2050, "model_basket_first": 12.4, "model_basket_last": 327.9, "model_basket_multiple": 26.44, "model_basket_cagr": 0.0333}.

The Answer

The model basket cost $12.40 in 1950, $186.20 in 2024, and $327.90 in 2050. The nominal multiple from 1950 to 2024 is about 15.02, and from 2024 to 2050 about 1.76. In 2024 dollars the 1950 basket cost about $161.40, so the basket became genuinely dearer in real terms from 1950 to 2024, while the 2050 model basket costs about $182.38 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 basket by design. The series is 1950: $12.40 model basket; 2024: $186.20 model basket; 2050: $327.90 model basket. From 1950 to 2050 the model basket price rose from $12.40 to $327.90, a multiple of 26.44 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.33% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. The 1950 and 2024 baskets are assembled from the public records' prices, so they inherit the gaps and the unit changes of those records.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 1950 to 2050 the model basket price rose from $12.40 to $327.90, a multiple of 26.44 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.33% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. The comparator uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. Scientific relation: Signature model deflator, design-study values (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0). The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. The cited background sources confirm the real historical anchors the design builds on; the model values themselves are checked only for internal consistency.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 12.4, 'model basket'), (2024, 186.2, 'model basket'), (2050, 327.9, 'model basket')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureThenVsNowProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1950, 12.4, 'model basket'), (2024, 186.2, 'model basket'), (2050, 327.9, 'model basket')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureThenVsNowProgram().run())

Signature Decade Price Atlas JAH-COTYS-000005 · compounded

History

The Signature Decade Price Atlas is a design for an atlas that maps the price of a loaf of bread across the decades, one page per decade, from nine cents in 1930 to $1.97 in 2024 and a model $3.47 in 2050. Each page shows the bread price for that decade, the price in 2024 dollars, and a short note on what was happening in the bread market. The 1930 and 2024 figures are real BLS prices from the public bread record, and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate.

Description

  • BLS Bulletin 1055 (1952) - Retail Prices of Food, 1950 (mirror) — https://vitrine.hraedon.com/archive/sources/bls-bulletin-1055-1952.html (BLS Bulletin 1055: bread 14.3 cents per pound in 1950, the atlas's mid-century anchor.)

History

The Signature Decade Price Atlas is a design for an atlas that maps the price of a loaf of bread across the decades, one page per decade, from nine cents in 1930 to $1.97 in 2024 and a model $3.47 in 2050. Each page shows the bread price for that decade, the price in 2024 dollars, and a short note on what was happening in the bread market. The 1930 and 2024 figures are real BLS prices from the public bread record, and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate.

Problem

How can the price of one everyday item be mapped across a century, so that a reader can open any decade and see what bread cost, what that meant in 2024 dollars, and what the model says for 2050?

Method

The design takes the real BLS bread prices from the public record for the historical decades, converts each into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, and adds one model page for 2050, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Each page carries the decade, the nominal price, the 2024-dollar price, and a note on the bread market of that decade. The series is 1930: $0.09 per loaf; 2024: $1.97 per loaf; 2050: $3.47 per loaf. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

Signature model deflator, design-study values (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0)

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. The cited background sources confirm the real historical anchors the design builds on; the model values themselves are checked only for internal consistency.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The model loaf cost $0.09 in 1930, $1.97 in 2024, and $3.47 in 2050. The nominal multiple from 1930 to 2024 is about 21.89, and from 2024 to 2050 about 1.76. In 2024 dollars the 1930 loaf cost about $1.69, so bread became genuinely dearer in real terms from 1930 to 2024, while the 2050 model loaf costs about $1.93 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 loaf by design. The series is 1930: $0.09 per loaf; 2024: $1.97 per loaf; 2050: $3.47 per loaf. From 1930 to 2050 the per loaf price rose from $0.09 to $3.47, a multiple of 38.56 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.09% per year.

Skeptical analysis

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. The atlas shows three pages only in this design; a full atlas would need a page for every decade, with the gaps in the butter and cheddar series stated on their own pages.

Specification

  • dimensions: model price in US dollars per per loaf; model years 1930-2050; Signature model deflator points
  • care: Never present a model value as a real historical price or a forecast; keep the design-study label on every display of the series.

Boundless scales

  • A2: The whole model series: the design across its decades.
  • A3: The design against the whole economy: model prices restated in 2024 dollars.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.09, 'per loaf'), (2024, 1.97, 'per loaf'), (2050, 3.47, 'per loaf')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureDecadeAtlasProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.09, 'per loaf'), (2024, 1.97, 'per loaf'), (2050, 3.47, 'per loaf')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureDecadeAtlasProgram().run())

Signature Cost-of-Living Index JAH-COTYS-000006 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Cost-of-Living Index is a Signature design-study for a cost-of-living index. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design sets the index to 100 in 2024, weights rent, groceries, transport, and utilities by their shares of a typical household budget, and grows the total at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The model household spent $1,850.00 a month in 2024, $2,108.03 in 2030, $2,620.51 in 2040, and $3,257.58 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Cost-of-Living Index is a design for an index that follows a household's monthly costs: rent, groceries, transport, and utilities, in one number. The design's index household spent $1,850.00 a month in 2024, and the model grows that to $2,108.03 in 2030, $2,620.51 in 2040, and $3,257.58 in 2050, all at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The index was designed as a Signature companion to the public rent and grocery records, which hold the real prices its shares are built from.

Description

The Signature Cost-of-Living Index is a Signature design-study for a cost-of-living index. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design sets the index to 100 in 2024, weights rent, groceries, transport, and utilities by their shares of a typical household budget, and grows the total at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The model household spent $1,850.00 a month in 2024, $2,108.03 in 2030, $2,620.51 in 2040, and $3,257.58 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a household's whole monthly cost be followed in one number, so that a reader can see whether life is getting dearer faster than wages, and how can the model years be shown without pretending they are measured?

The Worked Solution

The design fixes the household's basket of rent, groceries, transport, and utilities, weights each by its budget share, and prices the total each month. The total is shown in nominal dollars and in 2024 dollars, using the CPI-U for real years and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The series is 2024: $1,850 per month; 2030: $2,108 per month; 2040: $2,621 per month; 2050: $3,258 per month. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_month_first": 1850.0, "per_month_last": 3257.58, "per_month_multiple": 1.76, "per_month_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model household spent $1,850.00 a month in 2024 and $3,257.58 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model month costs about $1,812, so the model says the household's costs stay almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $1,850 per month; 2030: $2,108 per month; 2040: $2,621 per month; 2050: $3,258 per month. From 2024 to 2050 the per month price rose from $1,850 to $3,258, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real index would also need to handle the fact that households change what they buy, and that rent rises faster than groceries in the real record.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per month price rose from $1,850 to $3,258, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. import hashlib
class SignatureCostOfLivingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 1850.0, 'per month'), (2030, 2108.03, 'per month'), (2040, 2620.51, 'per month'), (2050, 3257.58, 'per month')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureCostOfLivingProgram().run())

Signature Household Budget Time Machine JAH-COTYS-000007 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Household Budget Time Machine is a Signature design-study for a budget time-travel tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a weekly budget, prices a fixed basket from the public records for any historical decade, and grows the budget forward to the model years at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The model budget was $212.00 a week in 2024, $241.57 in 2030, $300.30 in 2040, and $373.30 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Household Budget Time Machine is a design for a tool that takes a household's weekly budget and shows what it would buy in any decade: enter $212.00 a week, the design's 2024 anchor, and the machine shows the same budget as $241.57 in 2030, $300.30 in 2040, and $373.30 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and it shows what the 2024 budget would have bought in 1950 and 1970 from the real BLS prices. The time machine was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records, which hold the real prices it travels through.

Description

The Signature Household Budget Time Machine is a Signature design-study for a budget time-travel tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a weekly budget, prices a fixed basket from the public records for any historical decade, and grows the budget forward to the model years at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The model budget was $212.00 a week in 2024, $241.57 in 2030, $300.30 in 2040, and $373.30 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a household see what its weekly budget would have bought in 1950, what it buys in 2024, and what it would buy in the model years, without mixing the real prices with the model prices?

The Worked Solution

The design fixes one basket from the public records, prices it for any historical decade from the real BLS prices, converts the total into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, and grows the budget forward to the model years at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The historical pages are labeled as real, and the model pages are labeled as model. The series is 2024: $212.00 per week; 2030: $241.57 per week; 2040: $300.30 per week; 2050: $373.30 per week. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_week_first": 212.0, "per_week_last": 373.3, "per_week_multiple": 1.76, "per_week_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model budget was $212.00 a week in 2024 and $373.30 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model budget is about $207.63, so the model says the budget buys almost exactly as much in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $212.00 per week; 2030: $241.57 per week; 2040: $300.30 per week; 2050: $373.30 per week. From 2024 to 2050 the per week price rose from $212.00 to $373.30, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real time machine would also need to handle the gaps in the butter and cheddar series, not just the clean bread and milk series.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per week price rose from $212.00 to $373.30, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 212.0, 'per week'), (2030, 241.57, 'per week'), (2040, 300.3, 'per week'), (2050, 373.3, 'per week')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureBudgetTimeMachineProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 212.0, 'per week'), (2030, 241.57, 'per week'), (2040, 300.3, 'per week'), (2050, 373.3, 'per week')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureBudgetTimeMachineProgram().run())

Signature Price Memory Archive JAH-COTYS-000008 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Price Memory Archive is a Signature design-study for a price-memory archive. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design stores one price per year for a gallon of milk, with the real BLS prices for the historical years and model prices for 2030, 2040, and 2050. The model milk cost $3.98 a gallon in 2024, $4.54 in 2030, $5.64 in 2040, and $7.01 in 2050. History

The Signature Price Memory Archive is a design for an archive that keeps the price of a gallon of milk for every year, so that a reader can ask what milk cost in 1960, in 2024, or in the model year 2050, and get an answer with its source. The archive's model milk cost $3.98 a gallon in 2024, $4.54 in 2030, $5.64 in 2040, and $7.01 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and its historical pages hold the real BLS milk prices from the public record, including the unit change from half-gallon to gallon. The archive was designed as a Signature companion to the public milk record.

Description

Sources

  • Stacker - The Cost of Goods the Year You Were Born (BLS average prices) — https://stacker.com/stories/business-economy/cost-goods-year-you-were-born (BLS average retail prices of milk, 1930-2024, the background data for the archive.)
  • Taste of Home - This Is What Groceries Cost the Year You Were Born — https://www.tasteofhome.com/collection/this-is-what-groceries-cost-the-year-you-were-born/ (Grocery prices by birth year, cross-checking the BLS milk series.)

History

The Signature Price Memory Archive is a design for an archive that keeps the price of a gallon of milk for every year, so that a reader can ask what milk cost in 1960, in 2024, or in the model year 2050, and get an answer with its source. The archive's model milk cost $3.98 a gallon in 2024, $4.54 in 2030, $5.64 in 2040, and $7.01 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and its historical pages hold the real BLS milk prices from the public record, including the unit change from half-gallon to gallon. The archive was designed as a Signature companion to the public milk record.

Problem

How can the price of one item be kept for every year, with its source, so that a reader can ask about any year and get a sourced answer, and how can the model years be stored without looking like measured prices?

Method

The design stores one record per year, with the price, the unit, the source, and a flag that says whether the price is real or model. The historical years carry the real BLS milk prices from the public record, with the unit change from half-gallon to gallon stated on the 1998 page. The model years carry the model prices, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and flagged as model on every display. The series is 2024: $3.98 per gallon; 2030: $4.54 per gallon; 2040: $5.64 per gallon; 2050: $7.01 per gallon. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

Signature model deflator, design-study values (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0)

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. The cited background sources confirm the real historical anchors the design builds on; the model values themselves are checked only for internal consistency.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The model milk cost $3.98 a gallon in 2024 and $7.01 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model gallon costs about $3.90, so the model says milk stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $3.98 per gallon; 2030: $4.54 per gallon; 2040: $5.64 per gallon; 2050: $7.01 per gallon. From 2024 to 2050 the per gallon price rose from $3.98 to $7.01, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

Skeptical analysis

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real archive would also need to store the 1978-1979 gap, when BLS published no retail food prices, as a gap, not as a zero.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 3.98, 'per gallon'), (2030, 4.54, 'per gallon'), (2040, 5.64, 'per gallon'), (2050, 7.01, 'per gallon')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignaturePriceMemoryProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 3.98, 'per gallon'), (2030, 4.54, 'per gallon'), (2040, 5.64, 'per gallon'), (2050, 7.01, 'per gallon')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignaturePriceMemoryProgram().run())

Signature Price Alert Engine JAH-COTYS-000009 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Price Alert Engine is a Signature design-study for a price-alert engine. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design watches one item, a dozen eggs, and fires an alert when the price jumps by more than ten percent in a month or twenty percent in a year, with the alert carrying the old price, the new price, and the reason the engine knows. The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050.
