History

The Signature Price Alert Engine is a design for an engine that watches the price of a dozen eggs and alerts a household when the price jumps by more than ten percent in a month, or by more than twenty percent in a year. The engine's model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and its historical pages hold the real BLS egg prices, including the 2015 and 2022 spikes that would have triggered its alerts. The engine was designed as a Signature companion to the public egg record.

Description

The Signature Price Alert Engine is a Signature design-study for a price-alert engine. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design watches one item, a dozen eggs, and fires an alert when the price jumps by more than ten percent in a month or twenty percent in a year, with the alert carrying the old price, the new price, and the reason the engine knows. The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a household be warned when the price of eggs jumps, before the weekly shop, and how can the alert be useful without crying wolf every time the price wobbles by a few cents?

The Worked Solution

The design sets two thresholds: a ten percent monthly jump and a twenty percent annual jump, and it fires an alert only when a threshold is crossed, with the old price, the new price, and the percentage change. The thresholds were chosen from the real egg record, where the 2015 and 2022 spikes both crossed the annual threshold and the ordinary monthly wobble did not. The series is 2024: $3.17 per dozen; 2030: $3.61 per dozen; 2040: $4.49 per dozen; 2050: $5.58 per dozen. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_dozen_first": 3.17, "per_dozen_last": 5.58, "per_dozen_multiple": 1.76, "per_dozen_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024 and $5.58 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model dozen costs about $3.10, so the model says eggs stay almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $3.17 per dozen; 2030: $3.61 per dozen; 2040: $4.49 per dozen; 2050: $5.58 per dozen. From 2024 to 2050 the per dozen price rose from $3.17 to $5.58, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real alert engine would also need to handle the 2022 avian-influenza spike, which was a supply shock, not a trend, and which the engine should report as a shock.

From 2024 to 2050 the per dozen price rose from $3.17 to $5.58, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. Full working program

import hashlib
class SignaturePriceAlertProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 3.17, 'per dozen'), (2030, 3.61, 'per dozen'), (2040, 4.49, 'per dozen'), (2050, 5.58, 'per dozen')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignaturePriceAlertProgram().run())

Signature CPI Explorer JAH-COTYS-000010 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature CPI Explorer is a Signature design-study for a CPI-exploration tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes one item, a pound of ground coffee, and shows its price beside the CPI-U for every year, so a reader can see when the item's price rose faster than the index and when it rose slower. The model coffee cost $6.32 a pound in 2024, $7.20 in 2030, $8.95 in 2040, and $11.13 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature CPI Explorer is a design for a tool that explains the Consumer Price Index through one item, a pound of ground coffee. The explorer shows that coffee cost $6.32 a pound in 2024, $7.20 in 2030, $8.95 in 2040, and $11.13 in 2050 in the model, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and it shows the real CPI-U beside each price, so a reader can see that the index and the coffee price rise together in the model but moved apart in real history, when coffee fell in real terms from 1980 to 1990. The explorer was designed as a Signature companion to the public coffee record.

Description

The Signature CPI Explorer is a Signature design-study for a CPI-exploration tool. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes one item, a pound of ground coffee, and shows its price beside the CPI-U for every year, so a reader can see when the item's price rose faster than the index and when it rose slower. The model coffee cost $6.32 a pound in 2024, $7.20 in 2030, $8.95 in 2040, and $11.13 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a reader understand what the Consumer Price Index is, by watching one item's price move beside it, and how can the model years be shown without pretending the index is forecast?

The Worked Solution

The design pairs each coffee price with the CPI-U for the same year, and shows the coffee price in 2024 dollars beside the nominal price, so the reader sees the index doing its work. For historical years it uses the real CPI-U and the real coffee price; for projected years it uses the Signature model deflator and the model coffee price. The series is 2024: $6.32 per lb; 2030: $7.20 per lb; 2040: $8.95 per lb; 2050: $11.13 per lb. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_lb_first": 6.32, "per_lb_last": 11.13, "per_lb_multiple": 1.76, "per_lb_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model coffee cost $6.32 a pound in 2024 and $11.13 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model pound costs about $6.19, so the model says coffee stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $6.32 per lb; 2030: $7.20 per lb; 2040: $8.95 per lb; 2050: $11.13 per lb. From 2024 to 2050 the per lb price rose from $6.32 to $11.13, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real explorer would also need to show that the CPI-U is an average of hundreds of items, and that any single item, like coffee, can move far from the average.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per lb price rose from $6.32 to $11.13, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 6.32, 'per lb'), (2030, 7.2, 'per lb'), (2040, 8.95, 'per lb'), (2050, 11.13, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureCPIExplorerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 6.32, 'per lb'), (2030, 7.2, 'per lb'), (2040, 8.95, 'per lb'), (2050, 11.13, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')

                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}

            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out

        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Wage-vs-Price Gauge JAH-COTYS-000011 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Wage-vs-Price Gauge is a Signature design-study for a wage-versus-price gauge. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes the hourly minimum wage, grows it forward in the model at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and shows beside it how many loaves of bread, gallons of milk, and dozens of eggs one hour of work buys in each year. The model wage was $7.25 an hour in 2024, $8.26 in 2030, $10.27 in 2040, and $12.77 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Wage-vs-Price Gauge is a design for a gauge that shows what an hour of minimum-wage work buys: in 2024 the federal minimum wage was $7.25 an hour, and the gauge's model grows it to $8.26 in 2030, $10.27 in 2040, and $12.77 in 2050, all at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The gauge also shows the real history: the wage was $0.25 in 1938, peaked in real terms in 1968, and has been frozen at $7.25 since July 24, 2009, the longest freeze in the law's history. The gauge was designed as a Signature companion to the public minimum-wage record.

Description

The Signature Wage-vs-Price Gauge is a Signature design-study for a wage-versus-price gauge. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The Problem

How can a reader see whether wages are keeping up with prices, by counting what one hour of work buys, and how can the model years be shown without pretending the wage will rise?

The Worked Solution

The design divides the price of bread, milk, and eggs by the hourly wage for each year, so the gauge reads in items per hour of work. For historical years it uses the real wage and the real prices from the public records; for projected years it uses the model wage and the model prices, grown at the same steady 2.2 percent design rate. The series is 2024: $7.25 per hour; 2030: $8.26 per hour; 2040: $10.27 per hour; 2050: $12.77 per hour. The Answer

History

The Signature Wage-vs-Price Gauge is a design for a gauge that shows what an hour of minimum-wage work buys: in 2024 the federal minimum wage was $7.25 an hour, and the gauge's model grows it to $8.26 in 2030, $10.27 in 2040, and $12.77 in 2050, all at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The gauge also shows the real history: the wage was $0.25 in 1938, peaked in real terms in 1968, and has been frozen at $7.25 since July 24, 2009, the longest freeze in the law's history. The gauge was designed as a Signature companion to the public minimum-wage record.

Problem

How can a reader see whether wages are keeping up with prices, by counting what one hour of work buys, and how can the model years be shown without pretending the wage will rise?

Method

The design divides the price of bread, milk, and eggs by the hourly wage for each year, so the gauge reads in items per hour of work. For historical years it uses the real wage and the real prices from the public records; for projected years it uses the model wage and the model prices, grown at the same steady 2.2 percent design rate. The series is 2024: $7.25 per hour; 2030: $8.26 per hour; 2040: $10.27 per hour; 2050: $12.77 per hour. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Results reported

The model wage was $7.25 an hour in 2024 and $12.77 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model wage is about $7.10 an hour, so the model says the wage stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $7.25 per hour; 2030: $8.26 per hour; 2040: $10.27 per hour; 2050: $12.77 per hour. From 2024 to 2050 the per hour price rose from $7.25 to $12.77, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

Skeptical analysis

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real wages are set by law and by the labor market, not by a smooth rate, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real gauge would also need to show that the federal minimum wage has been frozen since 2009 while the model grows it every year.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2030, 8.26, 'per hour'), (2040, 10.27, 'per hour'), (2050, 12.77, 'per hour')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

latest_year: 2050

per_hour_last: 12.77

Run 2 SHA-256: 8ee17a41ea5d3b906a84a169552373d5daaadf9b4346250409f8c066329f0c92

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureWageGaugeProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2030, 8.26, 'per hour'), (2040, 10.27, 'per hour'), (2050, 12.77, 'per hour')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureWageGaugeProgram().run())

Signature Cost Story Generator JAH-COTYS-000012 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Cost Story Generator is a Signature design-study for a story generator. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a shopping list, prices it from the real BLS prices of 1950 and 2024, projects it to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and writes a short story about the list in each year, naming the items and their prices. The 1950 and 2024 list figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Cost Story Generator is a design for a generator that writes a short story about a shopping list: the list cost $8.40 in 1950, $124.60 in 2024, and $219.42 in the 2050 model, where the 1950 and 2024 figures are assembled from the real BLS prices in the public records and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The story names each item, its price in each year, and what the list means: a 1950 shopper carried the list home in a paper bag for the price of a modern coffee, and a 2024 shopper pays fifteen times as much for the same list. The generator was designed as a Signature companion to the public price records.

Description

The Signature Cost Story Generator is a Signature design-study for a story generator. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes a shopping list, prices it from the real BLS prices of 1950 and 2024, projects it to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and writes a short story about the list in each year, naming the items and their prices. The 1950 and 2024 list figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a price series be turned into a story that a reader remembers, without letting the story's color run ahead of the sourced prices?

The Worked Solution

The design assembles one list from the real BLS prices of the public records for 1950 and 2024, converts each list into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, grows the 2024 list forward to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and then writes the story from the numbers, naming each item and its price in each year. The story never invents an item or a price; it only narrates the sourced figures. The series is 1950: $8.40 per list; 2024: $124.60 per list; 2050: $219.42 per list. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 3, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2050, "per_list_first": 8.4, "per_list_last": 219.42, "per_list_multiple": 26.12, "per_list_cagr": 0.0332}.

The Answer

The model list cost $8.40 in 1950, $124.60 in 2024, and $219.42 in 2050. The nominal multiple from 1950 to 2024 is about 14.83, and from 2024 to 2050 about 1.76. In 2024 dollars the 1950 list cost about $109.34, so the list became genuinely dearer in real terms from 1950 to 2024, while the 2050 model list costs about $122.05 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 list by design. The series is 1950: $8.40 per list; 2024: $124.60 per list; 2050: $219.42 per list. From 1950 to 2050 the per list price rose from $8.40 to $219.42, a multiple of 26.12 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.32% per year.

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureCostStoryProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1950, 8.4, 'per list'), (2024, 124.6, 'per list'), (2050, 219.42, 'per list')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureCostStoryProgram().run())

Signature Price Puzzle Game JAH-COTYS-000013 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Price Puzzle Game is a Signature design-study for a price-guessing game. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design gives one level per decade for the price of a standard candy bar, with three choices per level and a score that rewards close guesses. The bar cost $0.05 in 1950, $0.25 in 1980, $1.00 in 2000, $2.00 in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model. The historical figures are real prices from the public record; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Price Puzzle Game is a design for a game that asks players to guess the price of a standard candy bar in each decade: five cents in 1950, twenty-five cents in 1980, about a dollar in 2000, about two dollars in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model, where the historical figures are real prices from the public candy-bar record and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Each level shows the bar, the decade, and three choices, and a wrong guess costs a point while a right guess earns the price difference. The game was designed as a Signature companion to the public candy-bar record, whose shrinkflation story is the game's hardest level.

Description

The Signature Price Puzzle Game is a Signature design-study for a price-guessing game. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design gives one level per decade for the price of a standard candy bar, with three choices per level and a score that rewards close guesses. The bar cost $0.05 in 1950, $0.25 in 1980, $1.00 in 2000, $2.00 in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model. The historical figures are real prices from the public record; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can the history of prices be turned into a game that teaches it, without letting the game's fun run ahead of the sourced prices?

The Worked Solution

The design takes the real candy-bar prices from the public record for the historical levels, converts each into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U so the game can show the real change, and adds one model level for 2050, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Each level shows the bar, the decade, and three choices, and the score rewards a close guess more than a lucky one. The series is 1950: $0.05 per bar; 1980: $0.25 per bar; 2000: $1.00 per bar; 2024: $2.00 per bar; 2050: $3.52 per bar. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 5, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2050, "per_bar_first": 0.05, "per_bar_last": 3.52, "per_bar_multiple": 70.4, "per_bar_cagr": 0.0435}.

The Answer

The model bar cost $0.05 in 1950 and $3.52 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 70.40 over one hundred years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 4.3 percent. In 2024 dollars the 1950 bar cost about $0.65, so the bar became genuinely dearer in real terms over a century, while the 2050 model bar costs about $1.96 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 bar by design. The series is 1950: $0.05 per bar; 1980: $0.25 per bar; 2000: $1.00 per bar; 2024: $2.00 per bar; 2050: $3.52 per bar. From 1950 to 2050 the per bar price rose from $0.05 to $3.52, a multiple of 70.4 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.35% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. The 2000 and 2024 figures are the labeled approximations from the public record, not BLS averages, and the game must say so on those levels.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 1950 to 2050 the per bar price rose from $0.05 to $3.52, a multiple of 70.4 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.35% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar restatement for every model price from the key figures. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 0.05, 'per bar'), (1980, 0.25, 'per bar'), (2000, 1.0, 'per bar'), (2024, 2.0, 'per bar'), (2050, 3.52, 'per bar')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignaturePricePuzzleProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1950, 0.05, 'per bar'), (1980, 0.25, 'per bar'), (2000, 1.0, 'per bar'), (2024, 2.0, 'per bar'), (2050, 3.52, 'per bar')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignaturePricePuzzleProgram().run())

Signature Historical Shopping List JAH-COTYS-000014 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Historical Shopping List is a Signature design-study for a historical shopping list. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes one list of eight groceries, prices it from the real BLS prices of 1960 and 2024, projects it to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and shows each item's price in each year beside the total. The 1960 and 2024 list figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Historical Shopping List is a design for a shopping list that prices the same groceries in 1960, 2024, and 2050: the list cost $14.80 in 1960, $156.88 in 2024, and $276.27 in the 2050 model, where the 1960 and 2024 figures are assembled from the real BLS prices in the public records and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The list holds a loaf of bread, a gallon of milk, a dozen eggs, a pound of bacon, a pound of ground beef, a pound of butter, a pound of cheddar, and a pound of sugar, and the design shows each item's price in each year beside the total. The list was designed as a Signature companion to the public grocery records.

Description

The Signature Historical Shopping List is a Signature design-study for a historical shopping list. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design takes one list of eight groceries, prices it from the real BLS prices of 1960 and 2024, projects it to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and shows each item's price in each year beside the total. The 1960 and 2024 list figures are assembled from real public prices; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can one shopping list be priced across sixty years, so that a reader can see which items rose fastest and which fell in real terms, without mixing the real prices with the model price?

The Worked Solution

The design fixes one list of eight groceries from the public records, prices each item from the real BLS prices for 1960 and 2024, converts each list into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, and grows the 2024 list forward to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Each item's price is shown in each year, so the reader can see that sugar fell in real terms while beef rose. The series is 1960: $14.80 per list; 2024: $156.88 per list; 2050: $276.27 per list. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 3, "earliest_year": 1960, "latest_year": 2050, "per_list_first": 14.8, "per_list_last": 276.27, "per_list_multiple": 18.67, "per_list_cagr": 0.0331}.

The Answer

The model list cost $14.80 in 1960, $156.88 in 2024, and $276.27 in 2050. The nominal multiple from 1960 to 2024 is about 10.60, and from 2024 to 2050 about 1.76. In 2024 dollars the 1960 list cost about $156.85, so the list cost almost exactly the same in real terms in 2024 as in 1960, while the 2050 model list costs about $153.66 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 list by design. The series is 1960: $14.80 per list; 2024: $156.88 per list; 2050: $276.27 per list. From 1960 to 2050 the per list price rose from $14.80 to $276.27, a multiple of 18.67 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.31% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. The 1960 and 2024 lists are assembled from the public records' prices, so they inherit the gaps and the unit changes of those records, and the butter price for 1960 is a gap, not a figure.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 1960 to 2050 the per list price rose from $14.80 to $276.27, a multiple of 18.67 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.31% per year. History

The Signature Historical Shopping List is a design for a shopping list that prices the same groceries in 1960, 2024, and 2050: the list cost $14.80 in 1960, $156.88 in 2024, and $276.27 in the 2050 model, where the 1960 and 2024 figures are assembled from the real BLS prices in the public records and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The list holds a loaf of bread, a gallon of milk, a dozen eggs, a pound of bacon, a pound of ground beef, a pound of butter, a pound of cheddar, and a pound of sugar, and the design shows each item's price in each year beside the total. The list was designed as a Signature companion to the public grocery records.

Problem

How can one shopping list be priced across sixty years, so that a reader can see which items rose fastest and which fell in real terms, without mixing the real prices with the model price?

Method

The design fixes one list of eight groceries from the public records, prices each item from the real BLS prices for 1960 and 2024, converts each list into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U, and grows the 2024 list forward to 2050 at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. Each item's price is shown in each year, so the reader can see that sugar fell in real terms while beef rose. The series is 1960: $14.80 per list; 2024: $156.88 per list; 2050: $276.27 per list. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Verification path

Results reported

The model list cost $14.80 in 1960, $156.88 in 2024, and $276.27 in 2050. The nominal multiple from 1960 to 2024 is about 10.60, and from 2024 to 2050 about 1.76. In 2024 dollars the 1960 list cost about $156.85, so the list cost almost exactly the same in real terms in 2024 as in 1960, while the 2050 model list costs about $153.66 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 list by design. The series is 1960: $14.80 per list; 2024: $156.88 per list; 2050: $276.27 per list. From 1960 to 2050 the per list price rose from $14.80 to $276.27, a multiple of 18.67 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.31% per year.

Skeptical analysis

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1960, 14.8, 'per list'), (2024, 156.88, 'per list'), (2050, 276.27, 'per list')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureShoppingListProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1960, 14.8, 'per list'), (2024, 156.88, 'per list'), (2050, 276.27, 'per list')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureShoppingListProgram().run())

Signature Cost Museum JAH-COTYS-000015 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Cost Museum is a Signature design-study for a museum exhibit. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design gives one display per decade for the price of a bottle of Coca-Cola, with the nickel vending machine as its centerpiece and the 2-liter bottle's price as its second room. The bottle cost $0.05 in 1930, $0.10 in 1970, $2.00 in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model. History

The Signature Cost Museum is a design for a museum exhibit that shows the price of a bottle of Coca-Cola across the decades: five cents in 1930, ten cents in 1970, about two dollars in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model, where the historical figures are real prices from the public Coca-Cola record and only the 2050 figure is a model value, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The exhibit's centerpiece is a vending machine that takes a single nickel, with a plaque explaining why the nickel held for seventy-three years, and a second plaque showing the 2-liter bottle's price from 2002 to 2013. The museum was designed as a Signature companion to the public Coca-Cola record.

Description

The Signature Cost Museum is a Signature design-study for a museum exhibit. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design gives one display per decade for the price of a bottle of Coca-Cola, with the nickel vending machine as its centerpiece and the 2-liter bottle's price as its second room. The bottle cost $0.05 in 1930, $0.10 in 1970, $2.00 in 2024, and $3.52 in the 2050 model. The historical figures are real prices from the public record; the 2050 figure is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a museum show the history of a price, so that a visitor understands why a bottle cost a nickel for seventy-three years and what happened when the fixed price ended, without presenting the model year as a fact?

The Worked Solution

The design gives one display per decade, with the real bottle prices from the public record, the price in 2024 dollars beside each, and a plaque on the nickel's three locks: the bottling contracts, the single-coin vending machines, and the advertising that printed the price. The 2050 display is marked as a model, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The series is 1930: $0.05 per bottle; 1970: $0.10 per bottle; 2024: $2.00 per bottle; 2050: $3.52 per bottle. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The Answer

The model bottle cost $0.05 in 1930 and $3.52 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 70.40 over one hundred twenty years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 3.6 percent. In 2024 dollars the 1930 bottle cost about $0.94, so the bottle became genuinely dearer in real terms over a century, while the 2050 model bottle costs about $1.96 in 2024 dollars, close to the 2024 bottle by design. Limits

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.05, 'per bottle'), (1970, 0.1, 'per bottle'), (2024, 2.0, 'per bottle'), (2050, 3.52, 'per bottle')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureCostMuseumProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.05, 'per bottle'), (1970, 0.1, 'per bottle'), (2024, 2.0, 'per bottle'), (2050, 3.52, 'per bottle')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureCostMuseumProgram().run())

Signature Price Predictor JAH-COTYS-000016 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Price Predictor is a Signature design-study for a price-prediction tool. It is not a real product, it is not a forecast, and it is not a historical record. The design projects the price of a dozen eggs forward at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, shows the real BLS egg prices beside the projection, and prints a warning on every display: this is a design-study, not a forecast. The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Price Predictor is a design-study for a price-prediction tool that projects the price of a dozen eggs forward: $3.17 in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The design is labeled design-study throughout, because it is not a forecast: it shows what the price would be if it grew at a steady rate, and it shows the real history beside it, including the 2015 and 2022 spikes that no steady rate could have predicted. The predictor was designed as a Signature companion to the public egg record.

Description

The Signature Price Predictor is a Signature design-study for a price-prediction tool. It is not a real product, it is not a forecast, and it is not a historical record. The design projects the price of a dozen eggs forward at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, shows the real BLS egg prices beside the projection, and prints a warning on every display: this is a design-study, not a forecast. The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024, $3.61 in 2030, $4.49 in 2040, and $5.58 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a prediction tool be built that is honest about being a model, showing both the projection and the real history that proves the projection will be wrong?

The Worked Solution

The design projects the price forward at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, shows the real BLS egg prices beside the projection, and marks every projected figure as a model value. The design also shows the 2015 and 2022 spikes, where the real price jumped by more than a dollar in a year, as proof that no steady rate predicts eggs. The series is 2024: $3.17 per dozen; 2030: $3.61 per dozen; 2040: $4.49 per dozen; 2050: $5.58 per dozen. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_dozen_first": 3.17, "per_dozen_last": 5.58, "per_dozen_multiple": 1.76, "per_dozen_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model eggs cost $3.17 a dozen in 2024 and $5.58 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model dozen costs about $3.10, so the model says eggs stay almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $3.17 per dozen; 2030: $3.61 per dozen; 2040: $4.49 per dozen; 2050: $5.58 per dozen. From 2024 to 2050 the per dozen price rose from $3.17 to $5.58, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. Eggs are the worst possible item for a steady-rate prediction, because avian influenza and feed shocks move the price by dollars in a year, and the predictor must say so on every display.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per dozen price rose from $3.17 to $5.58, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / D_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. class SignaturePricePredictorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 3.17, 'per dozen'), (2030, 3.61, 'per dozen'), (2040, 4.49, 'per dozen'), (2050, 5.58, 'per dozen')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():

            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')

                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0

Signature Cost-of-Things API Design JAH-COTYS-000017 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact.

The Signature Cost-of-Things API Design is a Signature design-study for a price API. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design defines three endpoints: a price for a year, the whole series, and the price in 2024 dollars, each returning the price, the unit, the source, and a flag that says whether the price is real or model. The model beef cost $5.39 a pound in 2024, $6.14 in 2030, $7.63 in 2040, and $9.49 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

History

The Signature Cost-of-Things API Design is a design for an application programming interface that serves the price of ground beef over time: one endpoint returns the price for a year, one returns the whole series, and one returns the price in 2024 dollars. The API's model beef cost $5.39 a pound in 2024, $6.14 in 2030, $7.63 in 2040, and $9.49 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and its historical endpoint serves the real BLS ground-beef prices from 1984 onward. The API was designed as a Signature companion to the public ground-beef record.

Description

The Signature Cost-of-Things API Design is a Signature design-study for a price API. It is not a real product and it is not a historical record. The design defines three endpoints: a price for a year, the whole series, and the price in 2024 dollars, each returning the price, the unit, the source, and a flag that says whether the price is real or model. The model beef cost $5.39 a pound in 2024, $6.14 in 2030, $7.63 in 2040, and $9.49 in 2050. Every value in this record is a model value made for the design, and every display of the series must keep the design-study label.

The Problem

How can a price series be served to other programs, so that a website, a spreadsheet, or a classroom tool can ask for the price of beef in any year and get a sourced answer, without serving a model price as if it were measured?

The Worked Solution

The design defines three endpoints, each returning the price, the unit, the source, and a real-or-model flag. The historical endpoint serves the real BLS ground-beef prices from 1984 onward, with the pre-1984 gap stated as a gap, and the model endpoint serves the model prices for 2030, 2040, and 2050, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The series is 2024: $5.39 per lb; 2030: $6.14 per lb; 2040: $7.63 per lb; 2050: $9.49 per lb. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 4, "earliest_year": 2024, "latest_year": 2050, "per_lb_first": 5.39, "per_lb_last": 9.49, "per_lb_multiple": 1.76, "per_lb_cagr": 0.022}.

The Answer

The model beef cost $5.39 a pound in 2024 and $9.49 in 2050, a nominal multiple of about 1.76 over twenty-six years, which is a compound annual growth rate of about 2.2 percent, by construction. In 2024 dollars the 2050 model pound costs about $5.28, so the model says beef stays almost exactly as dear in real terms, because the model rate and the model deflator are nearly the same by design. The series is 2024: $5.39 per lb; 2030: $6.14 per lb; 2040: $7.63 per lb; 2050: $9.49 per lb. From 2024 to 2050 the per lb price rose from $5.39 to $9.49, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year.

When It Applies

When designing a price tool, a classroom exhibit, or a then-versus-now display that needs a clean, labeled model series to work from.

Limits

As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2% model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. As a design-study, this record proves nothing about real future prices. The steady 2.2 percent model rise is a smooth design assumption; real prices jump, stall, and fall, and no model series here should be quoted as a forecast. A real API would also need to serve the ground-chuck cross-check for 1980 as a separate item, not as part of the ground-beef series.

Math Proof

The same arithmetic as the public records, applied to the design's model series: the nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first; the compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1; a model price restated in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / D_year, where D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year (2024: 313.7; 2030: 381.0; 2040: 464.0; 2050: 564.0) or the real CPI-U for a historical anchor year. From 2024 to 2050 the per lb price rose from $5.39 to $9.49, a multiple of 1.76 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.2% per year. P_year is the model price in the stated year and unit; D_year is the Signature model deflator for that year, or the real CPI-U annual average for a historical anchor year; 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last model price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The design uses the CPI-U as its reference frame: the real CPI-U annual averages for historical anchor years, and the Signature model deflator for projected years. The deflator is a design assumption, stated in the method, not a measurement and not a forecast. History

The Signature Cost-of-Things API Design is a design for an application programming interface that serves the price of ground beef over time: one endpoint returns the price for a year, one returns the whole series, and one returns the price in 2024 dollars. The API's model beef cost $5.39 a pound in 2024, $6.14 in 2030, $7.63 in 2040, and $9.49 in 2050, all grown at the steady 2.2 percent design rate, and its historical endpoint serves the real BLS ground-beef prices from 1984 onward. The API was designed as a Signature companion to the public ground-beef record.

Problem

How can a price series be served to other programs, so that a website, a spreadsheet, or a classroom tool can ask for the price of beef in any year and get a sourced answer, without serving a model price as if it were measured?

Method

The design defines three endpoints, each returning the price, the unit, the source, and a real-or-model flag. The historical endpoint serves the real BLS ground-beef prices from 1984 onward, with the pre-1984 gap stated as a gap, and the model endpoint serves the model prices for 2030, 2040, and 2050, grown from the 2024 anchor at the steady 2.2 percent design rate. The series is 2024: $5.39 per lb; 2030: $6.14 per lb; 2040: $7.63 per lb; 2050: $9.49 per lb. The design projects prices forward from its 2024 anchor at a steady 2.2% nominal rise per year, a Signature design assumption, not a forecast. This is a design-study record: every value here is a model value made for the design, not a real historical price.

Dataset

Signature model series, design-study values; historical anchors from the cited public background sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(2024, 5.39, 'per lb'), (2030, 6.14, 'per lb'), (2040, 7.63, 'per lb'), (2050, 9.49, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Run 2 SHA-256: 5f0b5e78b73e52050cd016dfbbecc0582a48ba8f1da1012433c096647c3f2562

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureCostApiProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(2024, 5.39, 'per lb'), (2030, 6.14, 'per lb'), (2040, 7.63, 'per lb'), (2050, 9.49, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7, 2030: 381.0, 2040: 464.0, 2050: 564.0}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureCostApiProgram().run())
